A quantum Frucht’s theorem and quantum automorphisms of quantum Cayley graphs
Michael Brannan, Daniel Gromada, Junichiro Matsuda, Adam Skalski, Mateusz Wasilewski

Abstract
. We establish a quantum version of Frucht’s Theorem, proving that every finite quantum group is the quantum automorphism group of an undirected finite quantum graph. The construction is based on first considering several quantum Cayley graphs of the quantum group in question, and then providing a method to systematically combine them into a single quantum graph with the right symmetry properties. We also show that the dual Γ ^ \widehat{\Gamma} of any non-abelian finite group Γ \Gamma is “quantum rigid”. That is, Γ ^ \widehat{\Gamma} always admits a quantum Cayley graph whose quantum automorphism group is exactly Γ ^ \widehat{\Gamma} .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 46L89; Secondary 05C25, 20G42

1.
Introduction and motivation

The study of symmetries of graphs is a very natural and rich area of research, dating back to the origins of the graph theory, and obviously connected to the theory of permutation groups. The classical Cayley theorem says that every finite group is a subgroup of a finite permutation group (as it acts faithfully on itself), and the added combinatorial structure of graphs and their symmetries makes it possible to strengthen this result in a very satisfactory way. Indeed, let us recall the classical
Frucht’s theorem
from
[
Fru39
]
, which shows that the automorphism groups of graphs are universal among finite groups.

Theorem

(Frucht)
.

For every finite group
G
there exists a finite simple graph
\mathcal{G}
such that
G\cong\Aut(\mathcal{G})
.

The main idea of the proof of Frucht’s theorem is fairly straightforward. One first lists elements of our group, writing
G=\{g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}\}
, and defines a
directed
,
edge-coloured
graph
\mathcal{G}_{0}
whose vertex set coincides with the set
G
and the edge colours are given by
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
An ordered pair
(g_{i},g_{j})
is then an edge of
\mathcal{G}_{0}
with colour
k\in[n]
if
g_{i}g_{j}^{-1}=g_{k}
. Note that
\mathcal{G}_{0}
is simply a coloured version of the Cayley graph of
G
, with respect to the full generating set
S=G
. Alternatively, we can view each colour
k
as representing a separate Cayley graph corresponding to the (not necessarily generating) set
S=\{g_{k}\}
. Next, one can go on to show that
G=\Aut\mathcal{G}_{0}
. We will recall the statement and its proof below as Proposition
4.1
. In the second step one applies a procedure of creating a new simple (non-coloured) graph
\mathcal{G}
, whose automorphism group is the same as in case of
\mathcal{G}_{0}
. We recall Frucht’s construction in Section
5
and also formulate a new, alternative approach to proving this result in Propositions
5.1
,
5.3
.

Our new approach to Frucht’s theorem comes with two distinct advantages. Firstly, our construction is more efficient in the sense that it yields graphs
\mathcal{G}
with much fewer vertices than those provided by Frucht’s method. Secondly, our construction turns out to be more amenable to
quantisation of Frucht’s theorem
, which is the main goal of this paper. More precisely, we wish to understand to what extent Frucht’s theorem holds in the realm of
quantum symmetry groups
(see for example
[
ASS17
]
).
This problem was already addressed before, with certain non-universality results obtained. First, we may ask whether for every finite quantum group
\mathbb{G}
(viewed as an object described by a finite-dimensional CQG-algebra, see
[
DK94
]
, fitting also in the theory of Woronowicz
[
NT13
]
), there is a graph
\mathcal{G}
such that
\mathbb{G}
is its quantum automorphism group. This turns out to be false, as there are actually finite quantum groups not faithfully acting on any finite classical spaces – in other words, that do not appear as (quantum) subgroups of
quantum permutation groups

S_{N}^{+}

[
BBN12
]
. This can be reformulated by saying that a naive quantum version of the Cayley theorem fails. So, we might relax our expectation and ask, for any (finite) quantum subgroup of
S_{N}^{+}
, whether there is a graph that has the prescribed quantum automorphism group. Nevertheless, Banica and McCarthy showed in
[
BM22
]
that this is false as well. For instance, the Kac–Paljutkin quantum group introduced in
[
KP66
]
is finite, it acts faithfully on four points, but it is not an automorphism group of any finite graph.

From a certain viewpoint, the above no-go results are quite natural. Every finite group can be realized as a subgroup of a permutation group since it acts at least on itself. But for quantum groups, a problem appears already in this first step: every finite quantum group does act on itself, but it is itself not a classical set. Hence, in order to have a perfectly analogous situation for finite quantum groups, we must not only consider actions on sets, but also on
quantum sets
: finite-dimensional C*-algebras equipped with distinguished (tracial) states. Within the framework of quantum sets, there is a well-understood notion of a
quantum graph

\mathcal{G}
over a quantum set – see e.g.
[
MRV18
,
MRV19
,
Daw24
,
Was24
]
. Moreover, one can construct the
quantum automorphism group of
\mathcal{G}
,
\Qut(\mathcal{G})
, which is a compact matrix quantum group which generalises the usual quantum automorphism group of a graph
[
Daw24
,
MRV19
,
Bra+20
]
. In general
\Qut(\mathcal{G})
can be an infinite compact quantum group, even when
\mathcal{G}
is classical. This can easily be seen by taking
\mathcal{G}
to be a complete (quantum) graph. For more exotic examples, one can consider Hadamard graphs and related examples
[
Gro24
,
Gro23
]
, the many examples considered by Schmidt in
[
Sch20
,
Sch20a
]
, or the Higman-Sims graph - the latter yielding a quantum automorphism group whose dual is a discrete quantum group with property (T) (see
[
RV24
]
, where the authors point out that it can be deduced from a combination of results from
[
Kup97
]
,
[
Ara18
]
,
[
Edg19
]
).
At this time, we do not know if every compact matrix quantum group arises as the quantum automorphism group of a quantum graph quantum graphs.
Nevertheless, one can at least ask: Can every
finite
quantum group be realised as the quantum automorphism group of a quantum graph? The main result of our paper is that this is indeed the case, establishing a satisfactory generalisation of Frucht’s theorem.

Theorem A

(Quantum Frucht’s theorem, Theorem
5.5
)
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a finite quantum group. Then there is an undirected finite quantum graph
\mathcal{G}
such that
\mathbb{G}=\Qut(\mathcal{G})
. That is,
\mathbb{G}
acts faithfully on
\mathcal{G}
, and the canonical quotient map
C(\Qut(\mathcal{G}))\to C(\mathbb{G})
is an isomorphism of Hopf *-algebras.

For the proof of the quantum Frucht’s theorem, it is natural to try and find a way to quantise Frucht’s original arguments. However, two issues arise. Firstly, given a finite quantum group
\mathbb{G}
, there seems to be no straightforward way to ensure the existence of a single coloured quantum graph
\mathcal{G}_{0}
whose quantum automorphism group is exactly
\mathbb{G}
. Secondly, even if such a
\mathcal{G}_{0}
could be found, Frucht’s decolouring procedure involves the addition of edges to
\mathcal{G}_{0}
– a process which seems quite hard to effectively quantise. Instead, we develop a new approach to Frucht’s theorem which is much more amenable to quantisation of both groups and graphs. We present the main proof of the quantum Frucht’s theorem in Sections
3
–
5
. As a first step, given any finite quantum group
\mathbb{G}
, we construct in Proposition
4.3
a finite set of quantum graphs
\{\mathcal{G}_{i}\}
, for each of which the quantum vertex set is
\mathbb{G}
and such that
\mathbb{G}
is the intersection of their quantum automorphism groups. As a second step, we establish Theorem
5.4
, which provides a general procedure that allows one to combine the family
\{\mathcal{G}_{i}\}
into a single quantum graph
\mathcal{G}
over a
larger quantum vertex set which still has
\mathbb{G}
as its quantum automorphism group.

We remark here that there has been some recent partial progress towards proving a quantum Frucht’s Theorem in other works.
For example, in
[
DRS24
]
it was shown that every classical finite group can be realized as the quantum automorphism group of a classical graph (the authors call this a “weak quantum Frucht’s theorem”).
The key idea in their approach was to check that each step of Frucht’s original decolouration procedure does not introduce any new
quantum
symmetries. In another direction, the work
[
BH24
]
shows that certain finite quantum groupoids can be realized as quantum automorphism groupoids of “categorified graphs”. The latter are certain variations of quantum graphs modelled over finite index subfactors.

In both the classical and quantum Frucht’s theorems, a key role is played by (quantum) Cayley graphs and their symmetries. Indeed, these are the natural objects which support quantum group symmetry. An old problem in group theory, dating back to work of Watkins
[
Wat76
]
(see also
[
LS22
]
), is to classify the finite (or infinite) groups
G
which admit a Cayley graph
\Cay(G,S)
with few automorphisms, meaning that
\Aut(\Cay(G,S))=G
. Such Cayley graphs are also called
rigid
. In Section
6
, we initiate the study of this problem for finite quantum groups, and establish some partial results there. Most notably, we settle the problem completely for all duals of finite groups.

Theorem B

(Theorem
6.3
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a non-abelian finite group. Then the Pontryagin dual quantum group
\widehat{\Gamma}
admits a rigid quantum Cayley graph
\mathcal{G}
.
In other words, there exists a (directed) quantum Cayley graph structure
\mathcal{G}
on
\widehat{\Gamma}
such that
\widehat{\Gamma}=\Qut(\mathcal{G})
.

Applying the above result to finite perfect groups (i.e. those with trivial abelian quotients), we are able to easily generate many examples of quantum graphs with small classical automorphism group, and large quantum automorphism group.

Theorem C

(Theorem
6.4
)
.

Let
\mathbb{G}=\widehat{\Gamma}
, where
\Gamma
is a finite perfect group. Then there exists a quantum (Cayley) graph structure on
\mathbb{G}
such that the classical automorphism group is trivial, but the quantum automorphism group acts transitively.

The detailed plan of the paper is as follows: in Section
2
, we provide a brief introduction to quantum sets, graphs, and their quantum automorphism groups. Section
3
is devoted to a brief introduction to (finite) quantum Cayley graphs and makes connections to earlier work on this topic due to Vergnioux
[
Ver05
]
. In Section
4
we prove our coloured version of the quantum Frucht’s theorem, and the decolouring and combining procedure is presented in Section
5
. The final Section
6
is devoted to the study of rigid quantum Cayley graphs, and also studies several concrete examples related to duals of symmetric groups.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we discuss preliminary facts regarding quantum sets, quantum graphs, and their quantum automorphism groups.

2.1.
Quantum sets and quantum graphs

Classical and quantum finite graphs play a key role in our work. It will be convenient to present a uniform picture of both, so we begin with a rather non-standard approach to standard graphs.

Let
X
be a finite set. When we equip it with the counting measure,
\ell^{2}(X)
is the Hilbert space with orthonormal basis
(e_{x})_{x\in X}
indexed by elements of
X
.
A
(directed) graph
is then given by a pair
\mathcal{G}=(X,A)
of a finite set
X
of vertices and an adjacency matrix
A\colon\ell^{2}(X)\to\ell^{2}(X)
whose entries are
0
or
1
(in the canonical basis). This condition is equivalent to
A\bullet A=A
, where
\bullet
denotes the entrywise product of matrices, usually called the
Schur product
or the
Hadamard product
. The condition on the entries can sometimes be weakened, when weighted or coloured graphs are considered. Given
x,y\in X
if
A_{yx}=1
, we say that there is an oriented edge
x\to y
(note that most of the graph-theoretical literature uses a transposed convention). The graph is undirected if
A=A^{*}
, where
∗
denotes the usual adjoint (the conjugate transpose). The graph admits no loops if the diagonal of
A
is zero. If both these conditions are satisfied, the graph is called
simple
.

If
G
is a finite group, we denote by
\star
the group operation extended to
\ell^{2}(G)
linearly and call it the
convolution
. A Cayley graph of
G
with respect to a set
S\subseteq G
is the graph
\Cay(G,S)=(G,A)
, where
A_{gh}=1
if
g=kh
for some
k\in S
and zero otherwise. Equivalently, we can say that
Af=p\star f
for any
f\in\ell^{2}(G)
, where
p=\sum_{k\in S}e_{k}
. Note that one usually assumes that
S
is a generating subset of
G
.

The notion of quantum sets, sometimes called measured quantum sets, appeared for example in
[
MRV18
]
. A
finite quantum set
is a virtual object
\mathbb{X}
associated to a finite-dimensional (possibly non-commutative) C*-algebra
C(\mathbb{X})
, which is interpreted as the algebra of functions over
\mathbb{X}
. Given a tracial faithful positive functional
\psi
on
C(\mathbb{X})
, we may define an inner product
\langle x,y\rangle=\psi(x^{*}y)
,
x,y\in C(\mathbb{X})
. Denote by
m\colon C(\mathbb{X})\otimes C(\mathbb{X})\to C(\mathbb{X})
the multiplication map. It is well-known that there is a unique tracial positive functional
\psi:C(\mathbb{X})\to\mathbb{C}
such that considering the associated Hilbert space structure, we have
mm^{*}=\mathrm{id}
(in other words,
C(\mathbb{X})
admits a unique
tracial
1
-form
). We will always consider
\mathbb{X}
with the tracial
1
-form
\psi
and the associated inner product, and denote the particular Hilbert space by
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})
. The multiplication in
C(\mathbb{X})
will be denoted
\cdot
, the involution
∗
and the unit
1_{C(\mathbb{X})}
will be written as
\eta\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})
when we want to interpret it as a Hilbert space vector. For any operators
A,B\colon\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})
, we will define their
Schur product
by the formula
A\bullet B=m(A\otimes B)m^{*}
and denote by
\bar{A}
the operator acting by
\bar{A}f=(A(f^{*}))^{*}
for any
f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})
, calling it simply the
complex conjugate
of
A
. We will save the
*
-symbol for denoting the adjoint operation in a C
∗
-algebra. (In particular the
\ast
-operation on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})
is simply the adjoint operation inherited from
C(\mathbb{X})\cong\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})
). We will occasionally use diagrams to illustrate some complicated equations. For that purpose, we will use the following notation for the multiplication and unit:

. We can use this notation to express the Schur product as follows:

A key example of a finite quantum set of importance for our paper is given by a
finite quantum group
, i.e. a virtual object
\mathbb{G}
associated to a finite-dimensional CQG Hopf *-algebra (so in particular a
\textrm{C}^{\ast}
-algebra) denoted
C(\mathbb{G})
or
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{G})
. Finite quantum groups are a subclass of compact quantum groups, which will be discussed below. The bi-invariant, with respect to the comultiplication, positive-functional of
\mathbb{G}
, the so-called
Haar functional

h
, is, when properly normalized, the tracial
1
-form on
C(\mathbb{G})
, making
(C(\mathbb{G}),h)
a (measured) finite quantum set. Indeed, by
[
Wor87
, Proposition A 2.2]
the Haar functional on
C(\mathbb{G})
is given by
\bigoplus_{\alpha}n_{\alpha}\operatorname{Tr}_{n_{\alpha}}
, where
C(\mathbb{G})\simeq\bigoplus_{\alpha}M_{n_{\alpha}}
.

For the history and motivation behind the notion of a quantum graph we refer to the articles
[
Was24
]
and
[
Daw24
]
, where also alternative equivalent approaches are described. We will mostly focus on the setup described via quantum adjacency matrices. Thus a
(directed) quantum graph
is given by a pair
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{X},A)
, where
\mathbb{X}
is a finite quantum set and
A\colon\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})
, the
quantum adjacency matrix
, is a real Schur idempotent, i.e. it satisfies the equalities
A\bullet A=A=\bar{A}
. The quantum graph is called
undirected
if
A=A^{*}
, and it is said to have
no loops
if
A\bullet{I}=0
, where
{I}
stands for the identity. Note that if
A=\bar{A}
then (as we work with a tracial reference functional) we always have
A^{*}=\bar{A^{*}}
.
Several examples will appear below; for now just note that the edgeless quantum graph on
\mathbb{X}
is given by the map
A=0
, and the complete quantum graph (without loops) by the map
Af=\psi(f)\eta-f,

f\in C(\mathbb{X})
.

Given a directed quantum graph as above we define its
(in)degree spectrum
to be the spectrum of the operator
D_{A}:=m(A\eta\otimes{I}_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})}):\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})
. Likewise, we define the
outdegree spectrum
via the spectrum of the operator
D^{A}=m(A^{*}\eta\otimes{I}_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})})
. The eigenspace of
D_{A}
corresponding to an eigenvalue
d\in\mathbb{C}
is interpreted as functions supported on vertices of (in)degree
d
. The operators
D_{A}
and
D^{A}
clearly coincide if
\mathcal{G}
is undirected. Following
[
Mat22
, Definition 2.24]
we call a quantum graph
(\mathbb{X},A)

d
-regular
if
A\eta=A^{*}\eta=d\eta
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{C}
. A quantum graph
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{X},A)
is
d
-regular if and only if
D_{A}=D^{A}=d\,{I}_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})}
.

Proof.

If
(\mathbb{X},A)
is
d
-regular, then by definition
A\eta=d\eta
and hence
D_{A}=m(A\eta\otimes{I}_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})})=d\,m(\eta\otimes{I}_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})})=d\,{I}_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})}
. Similarly for
D^{A}
. On the other hand, if
D_{A}=d\,{I}_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})}
, then
d\eta=D_{A}\eta=m(A\eta\otimes\eta)=A\eta
.
∎

2.2.
Quantum symmetry/automorphism groups

In order to speak about quantum symmetries of (quantum) graphs, we actually need to introduce compact quantum groups because, in contrast with the classical case, even finite (quantum) graphs can admit infinitely many quantum symmetries. We will mostly consider an algebraic approach. A
compact quantum group
in the sense of Woronowicz is a virtual object
\mathbb{G}
associated to a
CQG-Hopf
*-algebra
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{G})
(for a definition of a CQG-Hopf *-algebra see
[
DK94
]
).
Practically all compact quantum groups studied in this paper will be
compact matrix quantum groups
, i.e. will admit a unitary matrix
U=(u_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}\in M_{n}(\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{G}))
(for some
n\in\mathbb{N})
such that
U
is a representation of
\mathbb{G}
, that is

\Delta(u_{ij})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}u_{ik}\otimes u_{kj},\;\;\;i,j=1,\ldots,n,

and the set of entries of
U
generates
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{G})
as a
∗
-algebra. We will then call
U
a
fundamental
representation of
\mathbb{G}
.

Observe that any finite quantum group
\mathbb{G}
as introduced in the previous subsection can be considered as a compact matrix quantum group. Indeed, the comultiplication can be interpreted as a map
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{G})\to C(\mathbb{G})\otimes\ell^{2}(\mathbb{G})
, so as a matrix acting on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{G})
with entries in
C(\mathbb{G})
, i.e. as a fundamental representation of
\mathbb{G}
.

Given two compact quantum groups
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{H}
we say that
\mathbb{H}
is a
(closed) quantum subgroup
of
\mathbb{G}
if there exists a surjective Hopf
∗
-algebra morphism
\pi:\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{G})\to\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H})
.
Similarly given matrix compact quantum groups
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{H}
with fundamental representations of the same size, we define their
intersection

\mathbb{G}\cap\mathbb{H}
to be given by the largest quotient
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{G}\cap\mathbb{H})
of both
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{G})
and
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H})
mapping generators to generators. That is,
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{G}\cap\mathbb{H})
is the universal *-algebra generated by
u_{ij}
subject to all the relations from both
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{H}
. One can check that this notion is compatible with the standard one, discussed for example
in
[
CHK17
]
.

For a detailed description of actions of compact quantum groups we refer to
[
De 17
]
. We will be solely concerned with actions on finite quantum spaces, where certain subtleties disappear and one can view the theory purely algebraically. So an
action of a compact quantum group
\mathbb{H}
on a finite-dimensional
\textrm{C}^{\ast}
-algebra

C(\mathbb{X})
is an injective unital
∗
-homomorphism
\alpha:C(\mathbb{X})\to C(\mathbb{X})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H})
satisfying the
action equation
:

(\alpha\circ\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H})})\alpha=(\mathrm{id}_{C(\mathbb{X})}\otimes\Delta_{\mathbb{H}})\circ\alpha.

The action
\alpha
as above is said to be
faithful
if
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H})
is the Hopf
∗
-algebra generated by the set of slices
\{(\omega\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H})})\circ\alpha(a):a\in C(\mathbb{X}),\omega\in C(\mathbb{X})^{*}\}
. It is said to be
ergodic
or
transitive
if
the fixed point subalgebra is trivial:
\{x\in C(\mathbb{X}):\alpha(x)=x\otimes 1_{\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H})}\}=\mathbb{C}1_{C(\mathbb{X})}
.

If
\mathbb{X}
is a finite quantum set, so we have also a fixed positive faithful functional
\psi:C(\mathbb{X})\to\mathbb{C}
, we say that the action as above
preserves

\psi
if

(\psi\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H})})\circ\alpha=\psi(\cdot)1_{\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H})}.

In
[
Wan98
]
Wang showed that any finite quantum set
\mathbb{X}
admits a
quantum symmetry group
i.e. there exists a
universal
compact quantum group
\Qut(\mathbb{X},\psi)
acting on
\mathbb{X}
with the action preserving
\psi
; with the action automatically faithful. More information, in particular the meaning of universality can be found for example in
[
ASS17
]
, but see also Proposition
2.3
below.

We will mention one specific example, which is relevant for what follows: Let
\mathbb{G}
be a finite quantum group, and let
\psi
be the suitable scalar multiple of the Haar state of
\mathbb{G}
(so that
(C(\mathbb{G}),\psi)
is a finite quantum set, as explained in the previous subsection). Then the coproduct
\Delta:C(\mathbb{G})\to C(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{G})
(note that we use on purpose two different notations for the same algebra, to highlight different roles it plays) defines an action of
\mathbb{G}
on
C(\mathbb{G})
. The fact that the action preserves
\psi
is nothing but the invariance of the Haar state. As the coproduct action of
\mathbb{G}
on
C(\mathbb{G})
is faithful, we can view
\mathbb{G}
as a quantum subgroup of
\Qut(\mathbb{G},\psi)
.

The following is the key definition for our paper – for alternative versions and an extended discussion we refer to
[
Daw24
, Section 9]
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
(\mathbb{X},A)
be a finite quantum graph. An action of a compact quantum group
\mathbb{H}
on
(\mathbb{X},A)
is given by an action
\alpha
of
\mathbb{H}
on
C(\mathbb{X})
which preserves
\psi
and the adjacency matrix
A
, where the last condition is understood via the commutation relation:

(A\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H})})\circ\alpha=\alpha\circ A

(note that we view above
A
as acting on
C(\mathbb{X})
).

The following result is now easy to deduce (see for example
[
Daw24
, Proposition 9.9]
).

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
(\mathbb{X},A)
be a finite quantum graph. Then there exists a universal compact quantum group
\Qut(\mathbb{X},A)
acting (faithfully) on
(\mathbb{X},A)
(via a map
\alpha:C(\mathbb{X})\to C(\mathbb{X})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\Qut(\mathbb{X},A))
); the universality means that for any other action
\beta:C(\mathbb{X})\to C(\mathbb{X})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H})
of a compact quantum group
\mathbb{H}
on
(\mathbb{X},A)
there exists a unique Hopf
∗
-algebra morphism
\gamma:\mathcal{O}(\Qut(\mathbb{X},A))\to\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H})
such that

\alpha=\beta\circ\gamma.

The
quantum automorphism group

\Qut(\mathbb{X},A)
is a quantum subgroup of
\Qut(\mathbb{X})
.

Note that some authors use the notation
\Aut^{+}(\mathbb{X},A)
instead of
\Qut(\mathbb{X},A)
, and call it the
quantum symmetry group
of
(\mathbb{X},A)

The last statement of the above proposition implies in particular that
\Qut(\mathbb{X},A)
is a compact matrix quantum group (as so is
\Qut(\mathbb{X})
). Thus the definition above can be reinterpreted as follows (note that this approach was followed in
[
Bra+20
]
and in
[
Gro22
]
): if
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
is some orthogonal basis of
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})
,
\mathcal{O}(\Qut(\mathbb{X},A))
is the universal *-algebra generated by elements
\{u_{ij}:i,j=1,\ldots,n\}
subject to relations

(2.1)

\text{$U=(u_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}$ is unitary,}\quad m(U\otimes U)=Um,\quad U\eta=\eta,\quad UA=AU,

where
U
is treated as a matrix acting on
\ell^{2}(X)
with entries in
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{G})
and the equalities above have to be interpreted accordingly. Diagrammatically,

(2.2)

where thick lines denote the space
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{G})
, whereas the thin lines denote the space
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})
.

Note that the abelianisation of
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{G})
is simply the Hopf *-algebra
\mathcal{O}(G)
, where
G=\Aut(\mathbb{X},A)
is the compact matrix group of

classical automorphisms
of
(\mathbb{X},A)
.
These are precisely the *-automorphisms of
C(\mathbb{X})
that commute with
A
.
When
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{X},A)
is a classical finite graph,
\mathbb{G}=\Qut(\mathbb{X},A)
is the well-studied quantum automorphism group of the graph
\mathcal{G}
. In this case, the fundamental matrix
U=(u_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}
is a magic unitary, and
\mathbb{G}
is a quantum subgroup of the quantum permutation group
S_{n}^{+}
. See
[
BBC07
]
for a survey on these quantum groups.

It is easy to see that if
(\mathbb{X},A)
is a complete or edgeless quantum graph, then
\Qut(\mathbb{X},A)=\Qut(\mathbb{X})
(the commutation relation with
A
is then automatic). For more examples we refer to
[
Gro22
]
.

Remark 2.4
.

Suppose that we are given a family
(\mathbb{X},A_{i})_{i\in I}
of quantum graphs on the same finite quantum set
\mathbb{X}
. It is then easy to see that we can make sense of
\bigcap_{i\in I}\Qut(\mathbb{X},A_{i})
(for example as a quantum subgroup of
\Qut(\mathbb{X},\psi)
), and it is a universal compact quantum group acting on
C(\mathbb{X})
with the action preserving
\psi
and each of the quantum adjacency matrices
A_{i}
. Classically this construction can be described as the study of symmetries of an
edge-coloured
graph, with the adjacency matrix
A_{i}
corresponding to a colour
i
; since we want the edges to be given a single colour, this requires an extra disjointness assumption about the
A_{i}
’s, namely that they satisfy
A_{i}\bullet A_{j}=\delta_{ij}A_{i}
. In the quantum setting we will be forced to work with more general families of quantum adjacency matrices, not necessarily ones consisting of mutually Schur-orthogonal maps. We will also comment in Subsection
4.1
on when we can work with genuine coloured quantum graphs.

Finally we mention an easy result which will be of use later. Whenever we have an action
\alpha:C(\mathbb{X})\to C(\mathbb{X})\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
, we get an induced map
\widetilde{\alpha}:\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
, so all the usual notions for actions extend to the level of
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{X})
1
1

1

Since we are dealing with finite-dimensional objects, it is just a matter of identification.
In the infinite-dimensional case, we would have to work with the Koopman representation, which is particularly nice when the action is trace-preserving.
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Given a quantum graph
(\mathbb{X},A)
, the eigenspaces of
D_{A}
(and
D^{A}
) are invariant with respect to the action of
\Qut(\mathbb{X},A)
. That is, the projections on the eigenspaces of
D_{A}
(and
D^{A}
) are intertwiners of the fundamental representation
U
of
\Qut(\mathbb{X},A)

Proof.

It is well known that representations and their intertwiners of compact quantum groups form a monoidal
*
-category (like for ordinary compact groups). Denote by
\Hom(U,V)
the space of intertwiners between
U
and
V
. By definition of
\Qut(\mathbb{X},A)
, we have
m\in\Hom(U\otimes U,U)
,
\eta\in\Hom(1,U)
,
A\in\Hom(U,U)
, where
U
is the fundamental representation of
\Qut(\mathbb{X},A)
. Consequently,
D_{A}=m(A\eta\otimes I_{l^{2}(\mathbb{X})})\in\Hom(U,U)
. The projections on the eigenspaces of
D_{A}
are polynomials in
D_{A}
, so they also have to be in
\Hom(U,U)
.
∎

3.
Quantum Cayley graphs

We will now briefly discuss quantum Cayley graphs, as defined and studied in
[
Was24
]
(in a broader context of arbitrary, possibly infinite, discrete quantum groups).
Let
\mathbb{G}
be a finite quantum group, as introduced in the previous section.
The Hopf
∗
-algebra
C(\mathbb{G})
is, apart from the usual algebraic multiplication, equipped also with a convolution product (essentially transported from the dual quantum group via the Fourier transform). It will be easier to work directly with convolution operators attached to functionals on
C(\mathbb{G})
. Given
\omega\in C(\mathbb{G})^{*}
we can define

L_{\omega}=(\omega\otimes\mathrm{id}_{C(\mathbb{G})})\circ\Delta:C(\mathbb{G})\to C(\mathbb{G}),

R_{\omega}=(\mathrm{id}_{C(\mathbb{G})}\otimes\omega)\circ\Delta:C(\mathbb{G})\to C(\mathbb{G}).

Given an element
x\in C(\mathbb{G})
we define a functional
\psi_{x}:C(\mathbb{G})\to\mathbb{C}
,
\psi_{x}(y)=\psi(xy)
for
y\in C(\mathbb{G})
, where
\psi
is the Haar functional of
\mathbb{G}
, normalised to be a
1
-form. The convolution of two elements
x_{1},x_{2}\in C(\mathbb{G})
is defined as the density with respect to
\psi
of the functional
\psi_{x_{1}}\ast\psi_{x_{2}}
. It can be written more explicitly as
L_{\psi_{S(x_{1})}}(x_{2})
, where
S
is the antipode on
C(\mathbb{G})
. We define the convolution operator
x\star:=L_{\psi_{S(x)}}
.

Remark 3.1
.

In the case of finite quantum groups,
\psi
is tracial
[
Van97
]
and one can simply describe the convolution of
x_{1},x_{2}\in C(\mathbb{G})
as
x_{1}\star x_{2}=\overline{\Delta^{*}}(x_{1}\otimes x_{2})=\Delta^{*}(x_{1}\otimes x_{2})
since it holds for arbitrary
y\in C(\mathbb{G})
that

\displaystyle\langle(x_{1}\star x_{2})^{*},y\rangle=\psi_{x_{1}\star x_{2}}(y)=(\psi_{x_{1}}\ast\psi_{x_{2}})(y)=\psi^{\otimes 2}((x_{1}\otimes x_{2})(\Delta y))

\displaystyle=\langle x_{1}^{*}\otimes x_{2}^{*},\Delta y\rangle=\langle\Delta^{*}(x_{1}^{*}\otimes x_{2}^{*}),y\rangle=\langle(\overline{\Delta^{*}}(x_{1}\otimes x_{2}))^{*},y\rangle=\langle(\Delta^{*}(x_{1}\otimes x_{2}))^{*},y\rangle,

where the traciality of
\psi
ensures (by
[
Mat22
, Proposition 2.15]
) that
\Delta^{*}
is real if and only if
\Delta
is so, while the latter is one of the defining properties of
\Delta
. The other equality
\overline{\Delta^{*}}(x\otimes\mathrm{id})=L_{\psi_{S(x)}}
follows from

\overline{\Delta^{*}}=(\psi\circ m\circ\Sigma\otimes\mathrm{id}_{C(\mathbb{G})})(S^{-1}\otimes\Delta),

where
\Sigma
is the tensor flip.
One can check this equality by precomposing the mutual inverse maps

(m\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)
and

(m\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathrm{id}\otimes S\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)
to the identity of conjugate and transposed adjoint:

\overline{\Delta^{*}}=(\eta_{\mathbb{G}^{2}}^{*}m_{\mathbb{G}^{2}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{G}})(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{G}^{2}}\otimes\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{G}})(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{G}^{2}}\otimes m_{\mathbb{G}}^{*}\eta_{\mathbb{G}}),

where
C(\mathbb{G}^{2})=C(\mathbb{G})^{\otimes 2}
with
m_{\mathbb{G}^{2}}=(m_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes m_{\mathbb{G}})(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\Sigma\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{G}})
.
Diagrammatically, we have
